I mean, I'm not really sure. I haven't really stopped to notice if I'm feeling a specific way right in that exact moment before it happens. I guess I'm just always kind of stressed? 

Like, take my foreman, Dave. He’s been doing this for fifteen years, and yesterday he completely messed up the framing on a load-bearing wall. I had to stand there in the mud—because of course it was pouring rain—explaining the blueprints to him like he was a first-year apprentice. And the whole time, the client is texting me non-stop. I actually had to turn my phone on silent yesterday just to hear myself think over the generators.

I don't know if it makes me feel better, exactly. Maybe? Honestly, I don't really think about it like that, I just do it. It's kind of like... well, it's like when you're trying to fix a bad pour on a foundation, right? You just keep adding cement trying to level it out, but you're not really fixing the underlying issue, you're just making a bigger mess. 

Speaking of messes, you wouldn't believe the site I was at this morning. The plumbers came in to do the rough-in for the master bath, and they completely ignored the updated schematics I sent them on Monday. I mean, I emailed them, I texted the lead guy, I even printed out a physical copy and taped it to the framing. And what do they do? They run the pipes right through where the custom vanity is supposed to go. Now I have to get them back out here, which is going to push the drywall guys back, and the client is already breathing down my neck about the timeline because they want to host some big Thanksgiving dinner in a house that currently doesn't even have walls. I just... I don't understand how people can be so bad at their jobs. 

But yeah, I don't really know if eating makes me feel better. I haven't really thought about it.

I just keep going until I’m so uncomfortably full that I feel sick. They run the pipes right through where the custom vanity is supposed to go. Now I have to get them back out here, which is going to push the drywall guys back, and the client is already breathing down my neck about the timeline because they want to host some big Thanksgiving dinner in a house that currently doesn't even have walls. I just... I don't understand how people can be so bad at their jobs. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if eating makes me feel better. I haven't really thought about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel guilty after eating so much?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I don't really know if guilt is the right word. I guess I haven't really analyzed how I feel afterward, you know? I just feel... full. Uncomfortably full. 

But honestly, the thing that makes me feel guilty is when I have to tell a client that their move-in date is delayed again. Like, I have this one couple right now, they're expecting a baby in two months, and they are so stressed about getting into the new house. And I had to sit them down yesterday and explain that the custom windows they ordered—the ones they absolutely *had* to have—are stuck on a cargo ship somewhere in the Pacific. I felt terrible, but what am I supposed to do? I can't build the windows myself. And then the husband started asking if we could just board up the openings temporarily so they could move in. I had to explain to a grown man why we can't just slap some plywood over a giant hole in the wall and call it a day, especially with a newborn. It's like people lose all common sense when they're building a house. 

So yeah, dealing with that kind of stuff makes me feel guilty, I guess. But with the food? I don't know, I just haven't really thought about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, yeah, I guess that's the thing. Once I start on those evenings, it feels like I'm completely on autopilot. Like, I'm literally standing there telling myself to stop because I have an early crew start the next day, but I'm still going back to the kitchen for more. It's like I can't hit the brakes. 

Which is ironic, because I spend my entire day telling people to hit the brakes. Like this morning, I had a guy on the excavator who decided he didn't need to wait for the utility locate flags to be marked before he started trenching for the new sewer line. I saw him from across the lot and had to literally sprint through the mud waving my arms like a maniac to get him to stop. I mean, do you know what happens if you hit a main gas line? Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. 

But yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? I know I should stop, I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep digging anyway.

I guess... no, I don't really feel like I have a choice. It's exactly like I said, I'm just on autopilot. I'll be standing right there in the kitchen, physically telling myself, "You need to stop, you have to be on site at 5:30 AM to meet the concrete trucks," but my hands are just grabbing more stuff out of the pantry anyway.

The driver was furious, the dispatcher was yelling at me on the phone, but what am I supposed to do? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. It's like nobody pays attention to the details anymore. You have to double-check every single thing or people will just screw it up and expect you to deal with the fallout. 

But yeah, with the eating... it just doesn't feel like a choice. I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep going back for more.

weight or shape on self-evaluation.\n  manifestation: Since I started wearing more fitted work clothes and being up front at meetings, I catch myself judging the whole day by whether my pants feel tight or whether I think people can see my stomach.\n- key: S048\n  name: Compensatory_Behavior_Purging\n  description: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.\n  manifestation: A few times a month after those nights, I’ve gone into the bathroom and made myself throw up because I panic about undoing all the work I’ve put into looking put-together.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I start on those evenings, it feels like I’m on autopilot, telling myself to stop because I have an early crew start, but still going back to the kitchen for more.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating and inappropriate compensatory behaviors both occur, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Looking back over the last few months, the overeating-and-then-purging pattern has averaged about weekly, even if some weeks it happens twice and other weeks not at all.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up and doing my job, but I’ve started turning down some after-meeting food plans or rushing home because I’m worried the evening will get away from me.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since I changed how I present myself for the more visible supervisory role.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / first_line_supervisor_of_construction_trades_or_extraction_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating so much?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if guilt is the right word. I guess I haven't really analyzed how I feel afterward, you know? I just feel... full. Uncomfortably full. \n\nBut honestly, the thing that makes me feel guilty is when I have to tell a client that their move-in date is delayed again. Like, I have this one couple right now, they're expecting a baby in two months, and they are so stressed about getting into the new house. And I had to sit them down yesterday and explain that the custom windows they ordered—the ones they absolutely *had* to have—are stuck on a cargo ship somewhere in the Pacific. I felt terrible, but what am I supposed to do? I can't build the windows myself. And then the husband started asking if we could just board up the openings temporarily so they could move in. I had to explain to a grown man why we can't just slap some plywood over a giant hole in the wall and call it a day, especially with a newborn. It's like people lose all common sense when they're building a house. \n\nSo yeah, dealing with that kind of stuff makes me feel guilty, I guess. But with the food? I don't know, I just haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like you can't control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess that's the thing. Once I start on those evenings, it feels like I'm completely on autopilot. Like, I'm literally standing there telling myself to stop because I have an early crew start the next day, but I'm still going back to the kitchen for more. It's like I can't hit the brakes. \n\nWhich is ironic, because I spend my entire day telling people to hit the brakes. Like this morning, I had a guy on the excavator who decided he didn't need to wait for the utility locate flags to be marked before he started trenching for the new sewer line. I saw him from across the lot and had to literally sprint through the mud waving my arms like a maniac to get him to stop. I mean, do you know what happens if you hit a main gas line? Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. \n\nBut yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? I know I should stop, I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep digging anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a choice about whether or not to eat when you start eating on those evenings?\n\nPatient: I guess... no, I don't really feel like I have a choice. It's exactly like I said, I'm just on autopilot. I'll be standing right there in the kitchen, physically telling myself, \"You need to stop, you have to be on site at 5:30 AM to meet the concrete trucks,\" but my hands are just grabbing more stuff out of the pantry anyway. \n\nSpeaking of concrete trucks, you wouldn't believe the nightmare I had with the dispatcher yesterday. I ordered three trucks for a foundation pour, right? Specifically requested a 4000 PSI mix with a retarder because it was supposed to be 90 degrees out. Well, the first truck shows up an hour late, which means the crew is just standing around on the clock doing nothing. And then, when the driver finally hands me the ticket, I see they sent a standard 3000 PSI mix. No retarder. I had to reject the load right there on the spot. The driver was furious, the dispatcher was yelling at me on the phone, but what am I supposed to do? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. It's like nobody pays attention to the details anymore. You have to double-check every single thing or people will just screw it up and expect you to deal with the fallout. \n\nBut yeah, with the eating... it just doesn't feel like a choice. I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep going back for more.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the eating happens more often on days when you've had particularly stressful events at work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, maybe? I haven't really tracked it like that. I guess I'm always stressed at work, so it's hard to tell if it's worse on certain days. 

Like, take yesterday for example. I had to deal with the electrical inspector. This guy, I swear he wakes up every morning and decides how he's going to make my life miserable. We had a rough-in inspection for a custom build, and he failed us because one of the outlet boxes in the kitchen island was a quarter of an inch too low according to some obscure local code amendment that they just passed last month. A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. 

But as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. It just kind of happens when it happens, I guess. I haven't really noticed a pattern.

Like, I have this one couple right now, they're expecting a baby in two months, and they are so stressed about getting into the new house. And I had to sit them down yesterday and explain that the custom windows they ordered—the ones they absolutely *had* to have—are stuck on a cargo ship somewhere in the Pacific. I felt terrible, but what am I supposed to do? I can't build the windows myself. And then the husband started asking if we could just board up the openings temporarily so they could move in. I had to explain to a grown man why we can't just slap some plywood over a giant hole in the wall and call it a day, especially with a newborn. It's like people lose all common sense when they're building a house. \n\nSo yeah, dealing with that kind of stuff makes me feel guilty, I guess. But with the food? I don't know, I just haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like you can't control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess that's the thing. Once I start on those evenings, it feels like I'm completely on autopilot. Like, I'm literally standing there telling myself to stop because I have an early crew start the next day, but I'm still going back to the kitchen for more. It's like I can't hit the brakes. \n\nWhich is ironic, because I spend my entire day telling people to hit the brakes. Like this morning, I had a guy on the excavator who decided he didn't need to wait for the utility locate flags to be marked before he started trenching for the new sewer line. I saw him from across the lot and had to literally sprint through the mud waving my arms like a maniac to get him to stop. I mean, do you know what happens if you hit a main gas line? Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. \n\nBut yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? I know I should stop, I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep digging anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a choice about whether or not to eat when you start eating on those evenings?\n\nPatient: I guess... no, I don't really feel like I have a choice. It's exactly like I said, I'm just on autopilot. I'll be standing right there in the kitchen, physically telling myself, \"You need to stop, you have to be on site at 5:30 AM to meet the concrete trucks,\" but my hands are just grabbing more stuff out of the pantry anyway. \n\nSpeaking of concrete trucks, you wouldn't believe the nightmare I had with the dispatcher yesterday. I ordered three trucks for a foundation pour, right? Specifically requested a 4000 PSI mix with a retarder because it was supposed to be 90 degrees out. Well, the first truck shows up an hour late, which means the crew is just standing around on the clock doing nothing. And then, when the driver finally hands me the ticket, I see they sent a standard 3000 PSI mix. No retarder. I had to reject the load right there on the spot. The driver was furious, the dispatcher was yelling at me on the phone, but what am I supposed to do? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. It's like nobody pays attention to the details anymore. You have to double-check every single thing or people will just screw it up and expect you to deal with the fallout. \n\nBut yeah, with the eating... it just doesn't feel like a choice. I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep going back for more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you've had particularly stressful events at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I haven't really tracked it like that. I guess I'm always stressed at work, so it's hard to tell if it's worse on certain days. \n\nLike, take yesterday for example. I had to deal with the electrical inspector. This guy, I swear he wakes up every morning and decides how he's going to make my life miserable. We had a rough-in inspection for a custom build, and he failed us because one of the outlet boxes in the kitchen island was a quarter of an inch too low according to some obscure local code amendment that they just passed last month. A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. \n\nBut as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know if it's about the stress. I mean, I guess it could be? But like I said, I'm pretty much always stressed, so it's not like I can point to a bad day and say, "Oh, that's why I ate." It just feels like it's something I do. 

Honestly, if I wanted to eat every time I was stressed, I'd never stop eating. Like, this morning, the framing crew showed up short two guys. Apparently, one of them threw his back out over the weekend, and the other one just didn't bother to call in. So suddenly I'm looking at a schedule that's already tight, and I've got half a crew trying to lift these massive LVL beams into place for the second-floor joists. I actually had to jump in and help them muscle one of those things up the ladders because we didn't have the crane scheduled until tomorrow. My shoulders are still killing me. And then, right in the middle of all that, the homeowner decides to drop by unannounced for a "quick walkthrough." She's walking around in open-toed sandals, pointing at the framing and asking if we can just move a load-bearing wall two feet to the left so her kitchen island will have better flow. I had to smile and nod and explain that no, we can't just move a structural wall without getting an engineer to redraw the plans and pulling new permits, which would delay the project by at least three weeks. She looked at me like I was just being difficult on purpose. 

So yeah, the stress is just... it's always there. I don't know if the eating is really about that or not. It's just something that happens.

Well, the first truck shows up an hour late, which means the crew is just standing around on the clock doing nothing. And then, when the driver finally hands me the ticket, I see they sent a standard 3000 PSI mix. No retarder. I had to reject the load right there on the spot. The driver was furious, the dispatcher was yelling at me on the phone, but what am I supposed to do? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. It's like nobody pays attention to the details anymore. You have to double-check every single thing or people will just screw it up and expect you to deal with the fallout. \n\nBut yeah, with the eating... it just doesn't feel like a choice. I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep going back for more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you've had particularly stressful events at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I haven't really tracked it like that. I guess I'm always stressed at work, so it's hard to tell if it's worse on certain days. \n\nLike, take yesterday for example. I had to deal with the electrical inspector. This guy, I swear he wakes up every morning and decides how he's going to make my life miserable. We had a rough-in inspection for a custom build, and he failed us because one of the outlet boxes in the kitchen island was a quarter of an inch too low according to some obscure local code amendment that they just passed last month. A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. \n\nBut as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. It just kind of happens when it happens, I guess. I haven't really noticed a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens as a way to deal with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about the stress. I mean, I guess it could be? But like I said, I'm pretty much always stressed, so it's not like I can point to a bad day and say, \"Oh, that's why I ate.\" It just feels like it's something I do. \n\nHonestly, if I wanted to eat every time I was stressed, I'd never stop eating. Like, this morning, the framing crew showed up short two guys. Apparently, one of them threw his back out over the weekend, and the other one just didn't bother to call in. So suddenly I'm looking at a schedule that's already tight, and I've got half a crew trying to lift these massive LVL beams into place for the second-floor joists. I actually had to jump in and help them muscle one of those things up the ladders because we didn't have the crane scheduled until tomorrow. My shoulders are still killing me. And then, right in the middle of all that, the homeowner decides to drop by unannounced for a \"quick walkthrough.\" She's walking around in open-toed sandals, pointing at the framing and asking if we can just move a load-bearing wall two feet to the left so her kitchen island will have better flow. I had to smile and nod and explain that no, we can't just move a structural wall without getting an engineer to redraw the plans and pulling new permits, which would delay the project by at least three weeks. She looked at me like I was just being difficult on purpose. \n\nSo yeah, the stress is just... it's always there. I don't know if the eating is really about that or not. It's just something that happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's just something that happens.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think eating helps you cope with the stress you experience at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / first_line_supervisor_of_construction_trades_or_extraction_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know if it helps. I mean, maybe in the exact second I'm doing it? But usually, I just feel terrible afterward, so it's not a very good coping mechanism if it is one. 

Coping is just... I don't know. You should see how some of my crew copes with stress. Like my lead drywaller, Terry. He copes by taking these ridiculous smoke breaks every forty-five minutes. I caught him yesterday out by his truck when he was supposed to be mudding the master bath, just chain-smoking and screaming on the phone to his ex-wife about child support. And I'm just standing there in the doorway, waiting for him to wrap it up so we can actually stay on schedule for the painters who are coming Thursday. 

Which, by the way, the painters are a whole other headache. They always demand that the HVAC is running so the paint cures faster, but the HVAC guys haven't even installed the registers yet because the supplier sent the wrong finish. We specifically ordered brushed nickel, and they sent three boxes of oil-rubbed bronze. Who even uses oil-rubbed bronze anymore? It looks like it belongs in some 2005 McMansion. I literally had to load up forty heavy metal registers into the back of my truck and drive them back to the supply house myself, which killed two whole hours of my afternoon. 

So... yeah, I'm not really sure. I guess I haven't really thought about whether the eating actually helps with any of that. It's just kind of a blur.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Yeah, well, I guess I decided to come in because things have just been a little... off lately. I supervise a construction crew—mostly residential builds right now—and honestly, the delays lately are just a nightmare. We had this massive hold-up on drywall last week, and I spent practically half my day just arguing with the supplier on the phone. And then on top of that, I have to go to these neighborhood zoning meetings that just drag on forever. Like on Tuesday, this woman, Mrs. Higgins, complained for forty-five minutes about the noise from the excavators. It’s a construction site, you know? What does she expect? \n\nAnyway, sorry, um... what made me actually make the appointment is that after those really long days on site, or after one of those awful neighborhood meetings, I’ll come home and just... I end up eating way more than I ever meant to. I'll just stand in the kitchen and go through whatever’s there. Leftover takeout, bowls of cereal, bags of chips... I just keep going until I’m so uncomfortably full that I feel sick. It's just getting frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before you start eating excessively?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I haven't really stopped to notice if I'm feeling a specific way right in that exact moment before it happens. I guess I'm just always kind of stressed? \n\nLike, take my foreman, Dave. He’s been doing this for fifteen years, and yesterday he completely messed up the framing on a load-bearing wall. I had to stand there in the mud—because of course it was pouring rain—explaining the blueprints to him like he was a first-year apprentice. And the whole time, the client is texting me non-stop. I swear, people watch one season of some home renovation show and suddenly they think they know more than the general contractor. They want custom arches but don't want to pay for the structural engineer to approve it. It’s wild. I actually had to turn my phone on silent yesterday just to hear myself think over the generators. \n\nBut, um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, if I'm upset right before I eat. Yeah, I don't know, I just haven't really tracked my mood like that. It's hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you feel better after a long or stressful day?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it makes me feel better, exactly. Maybe? Honestly, I don't really think about it like that, I just do it. It's kind of like... well, it's like when you're trying to fix a bad pour on a foundation, right? You just keep adding cement trying to level it out, but you're not really fixing the underlying issue, you're just making a bigger mess. \n\nSpeaking of messes, you wouldn't believe the site I was at this morning. The plumbers came in to do the rough-in for the master bath, and they completely ignored the updated schematics I sent them on Monday. I mean, I emailed them, I texted the lead guy, I even printed out a physical copy and taped it to the framing. And what do they do? They run the pipes right through where the custom vanity is supposed to go. Now I have to get them back out here, which is going to push the drywall guys back, and the client is already breathing down my neck about the timeline because they want to host some big Thanksgiving dinner in a house that currently doesn't even have walls. I just... I don't understand how people can be so bad at their jobs. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if eating makes me feel better. I haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating so much?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if guilt is the right word. I guess I haven't really analyzed how I feel afterward, you know? I just feel... full. Uncomfortably full. \n\nBut honestly, the thing that makes me feel guilty is when I have to tell a client that their move-in date is delayed again. Like, I have this one couple right now, they're expecting a baby in two months, and they are so stressed about getting into the new house. And I had to sit them down yesterday and explain that the custom windows they ordered—the ones they absolutely *had* to have—are stuck on a cargo ship somewhere in the Pacific. I felt terrible, but what am I supposed to do? I can't build the windows myself. And then the husband started asking if we could just board up the openings temporarily so they could move in. I had to explain to a grown man why we can't just slap some plywood over a giant hole in the wall and call it a day, especially with a newborn. It's like people lose all common sense when they're building a house. \n\nSo yeah, dealing with that kind of stuff makes me feel guilty, I guess. But with the food? I don't know, I just haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like you can't control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess that's the thing. Once I start on those evenings, it feels like I'm completely on autopilot. Like, I'm literally standing there telling myself to stop because I have an early crew start the next day, but I'm still going back to the kitchen for more. It's like I can't hit the brakes. \n\nWhich is ironic, because I spend my entire day telling people to hit the brakes. Like this morning, I had a guy on the excavator who decided he didn't need to wait for the utility locate flags to be marked before he started trenching for the new sewer line. I saw him from across the lot and had to literally sprint through the mud waving my arms like a maniac to get him to stop. I mean, do you know what happens if you hit a main gas line? Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. \n\nBut yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? I know I should stop, I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep digging anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a choice about whether or not to eat when you start eating on those evenings?\n\nPatient: I guess... no, I don't really feel like I have a choice. It's exactly like I said, I'm just on autopilot. I'll be standing right there in the kitchen, physically telling myself, \"You need to stop, you have to be on site at 5:30 AM to meet the concrete trucks,\" but my hands are just grabbing more stuff out of the pantry anyway. \n\nSpeaking of concrete trucks, you wouldn't believe the nightmare I had with the dispatcher yesterday. I ordered three trucks for a foundation pour, right? Specifically requested a 4000 PSI mix with a retarder because it was supposed to be 90 degrees out. Well, the first truck shows up an hour late, which means the crew is just standing around on the clock doing nothing. And then, when the driver finally hands me the ticket, I see they sent a standard 3000 PSI mix. No retarder. I had to reject the load right there on the spot. The driver was furious, the dispatcher was yelling at me on the phone, but what am I supposed to do? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. It's like nobody pays attention to the details anymore. You have to double-check every single thing or people will just screw it up and expect you to deal with the fallout. \n\nBut yeah, with the eating... it just doesn't feel like a choice. I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep going back for more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you've had particularly stressful events at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I haven't really tracked it like that. I guess I'm always stressed at work, so it's hard to tell if it's worse on certain days. \n\nLike, take yesterday for example. I had to deal with the electrical inspector. This guy, I swear he wakes up every morning and decides how he's going to make my life miserable. We had a rough-in inspection for a custom build, and he failed us because one of the outlet boxes in the kitchen island was a quarter of an inch too low according to some obscure local code amendment that they just passed last month. A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. \n\nBut as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. It just kind of happens when it happens, I guess. I haven't really noticed a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens as a way to deal with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about the stress. I mean, I guess it could be? But like I said, I'm pretty much always stressed, so it's not like I can point to a bad day and say, \"Oh, that's why I ate.\" It just feels like it's something I do. \n\nHonestly, if I wanted to eat every time I was stressed, I'd never stop eating. I actually had to jump in and help them muscle one of those things up the ladders because we didn't have the crane scheduled until tomorrow. My shoulders are still killing me. And then, right in the middle of all that, the homeowner decides to drop by unannounced for a \"quick walkthrough.\" She's walking around in open-toed sandals, pointing at the framing and asking if we can just move a load-bearing wall two feet to the left so her kitchen island will have better flow. I had to smile and nod and explain that no, we can't just move a structural wall without getting an engineer to redraw the plans and pulling new permits, which would delay the project by at least three weeks. She looked at me like I was just being difficult on purpose. \n\nSo yeah, the stress is just... it's always there. I don't know if the eating is really about that or not. It's just something that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it helps. chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you've had particularly stressful events at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I haven't really tracked it like that. I guess I'm always stressed at work, so it's hard to tell if it's worse on certain days. \n\nLike, take yesterday for example. I had to deal with the electrical inspector. This guy, I swear he wakes up every morning and decides how he's going to make my life miserable. A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. \n\nBut as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. It just kind of happens when it happens, I guess. I haven't really noticed a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens as a way to deal with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about the stress. I mean, I guess it could be? But like I said, I'm pretty much always stressed, so it's not like I can point to a bad day and say, \"Oh, that's why I ate.\" It just feels like it's something I do. \n\nHonestly, if I wanted to eat every time I was stressed, I'd never stop eating. Like, this morning, the framing crew showed up short two guys. Apparently, one of them threw his back out over the weekend, and the other one just didn't bother to call in. So suddenly I'm looking at a schedule that's already tight, and I've got half a crew trying to lift these massive LVL beams into place for the second-floor joists. I actually had to jump in and help them muscle one of those things up the ladders because we didn't have the crane scheduled until tomorrow. My shoulders are still killing me. And then, right in the middle of all that, the homeowner decides to drop by unannounced for a \"quick walkthrough.\" She's walking around in open-toed sandals, pointing at the framing and asking if we can just move a load-bearing wall two feet to the left so her kitchen island will have better flow. I had to smile and nod and explain that no, we can't just move a structural wall without getting an engineer to redraw the plans and pulling new permits, which would delay the project by at least three weeks. She looked at me like I was just being difficult on purpose. \n\nSo yeah, the stress is just... it's always there. I don't know if the eating is really about that or not. It's just something that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it helps. I mean, maybe in the exact second I'm doing it? But usually, I just feel terrible afterward, so it's not a very good coping mechanism if it is one. \n\nCoping is just... I don't know. You should see how some of my crew copes with stress. Like my lead drywaller, Terry. He copes by taking these ridiculous smoke breaks every forty-five minutes. I caught him yesterday out by his truck when he was supposed to be mudding the master bath, just chain-smoking and screaming on the phone to his ex-wife about child support. And I'm just standing there in the doorway, waiting for him to wrap it up so we can actually stay on schedule for the painters who are coming Thursday. \n\nWhich, by the way, the painters are a whole other headache. They always demand that the HVAC is running so the paint cures faster, but the HVAC guys haven't even installed the registers yet because the supplier sent the wrong finish. We specifically ordered brushed nickel, and they sent three boxes of oil-rubbed bronze. Who even uses oil-rubbed bronze anymore? It looks like it belongs in some 2005 McMansion. I literally had to load up forty heavy metal registers into the back of my truck and drive them back to the supply house myself, which killed two whole hours of my afternoon. \n\nSo... yeah, I'm not really sure. I guess I haven't really thought about whether the eating actually helps with any of that. It's just kind of a blur.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually eat like this immediately after a stressful event at work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don't think it's like an immediate reaction, no. It's not like I get off a bad phone call and instantly go eat. It's more just... later, maybe? I don't really track the timing. 

Speaking of timing, though, you wouldn't believe the scheduling nightmare I'm dealing with for the concrete pour on this new foundation. We were supposed to pour on Tuesday, right? But the meteorologist decides to predict a ninety percent chance of thunderstorms, so I have to call the concrete plant and push it to Thursday. Well, Thursday comes, and the weather is beautiful, not a cloud in the sky—but the pump truck breaks down on the highway. Just completely blows a transmission or something. So the concrete is sitting in the mixer trucks, getting hot, and the drivers are getting antsy because if that stuff sets up in the drum, they have to literally jackhammer it out. I spent three hours frantically calling every other pump company in a fifty-mile radius, begging someone to send a truck out. I finally found a guy, but he charged me double the standard rate just because he knew I was desperate. And then the homeowner was upset because the noise was bothering their neighbor's dog. A dog! We're pouring a foundation, of course it's loud. 

So yeah, I don't really know if the eating happens right after something stressful. I just haven't noticed if there's a connection like that.

Honestly, I don't really think about it like that, I just do it. It's kind of like... well, it's like when you're trying to fix a bad pour on a foundation, right? They run the pipes right through where the custom vanity is supposed to go. I haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating so much?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if guilt is the right word. I guess I haven't really analyzed how I feel afterward, you know? I just feel... full. Uncomfortably full. \n\nBut honestly, the thing that makes me feel guilty is when I have to tell a client that their move-in date is delayed again. Like, I have this one couple right now, they're expecting a baby in two months, and they are so stressed about getting into the new house. I felt terrible, but what am I supposed to do? I can't build the windows myself. And then the husband started asking if we could just board up the openings temporarily so they could move in. It's like I can't hit the brakes. \n\nWhich is ironic, because I spend my entire day telling people to hit the brakes. Like this morning, I had a guy on the excavator who decided he didn't need to wait for the utility locate flags to be marked before he started trenching for the new sewer line. I saw him from across the lot and had to literally sprint through the mud waving my arms like a maniac to get him to stop. I mean, do you know what happens if you hit a main gas line? Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. \n\nBut yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. \n\nBut yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. \n\nBut as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. It just kind of happens when it happens, I guess. I mean, I guess it could be? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, I don't really know. Honestly, it's hard to even remember a day when nothing stressful happens. I guess maybe? But I couldn't tell you for sure. 

Like, even on days that are supposed to be "easy," something always comes up. Take last Friday. We were just doing final punch-list items on a remodel. It was supposed to be a half-day. But then the cabinet guy shows up to install the custom range hood, and he realizes he measured wrong. The whole thing was an inch and a half too wide to fit between the upper cabinets. And these are custom, inset shaker cabinets, painted in this specific shade of navy blue that took us three tries to get right. You can't just shave off an inch and a half without ruining the finish. So he's standing there scratching his head, and I'm having to call the client and explain why their kitchen won't be finished for another two weeks while we wait for a new hood to be fabricated. And of course, they had a dinner party planned for the weekend to show off the new kitchen. I ended up having to buy them a gift card to a nice steakhouse just to smooth things over, which came right out of my overhead. 

So, yeah... days without stress? I don't think those really exist for me, so I don't know if the eating happens on those days or not.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Yeah, well, I guess I decided to come in because things have just been a little... off lately. I supervise a construction crew—mostly residential builds right now—and honestly, the delays lately are just a nightmare. We had this massive hold-up on drywall last week, and I spent practically half my day just arguing with the supplier on the phone. And then on top of that, I have to go to these neighborhood zoning meetings that just drag on forever. Like on Tuesday, this woman, Mrs. Higgins, complained for forty-five minutes about the noise from the excavators. It’s a construction site, you know? What does she expect? \n\nAnyway, sorry, um... what made me actually make the appointment is that after those really long days on site, or after one of those awful neighborhood meetings, I’ll come home and just... I end up eating way more than I ever meant to. I'll just stand in the kitchen and go through whatever’s there. Leftover takeout, bowls of cereal, bags of chips... I just keep going until I’m so uncomfortably full that I feel sick. It's just getting frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before you start eating excessively?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I haven't really stopped to notice if I'm feeling a specific way right in that exact moment before it happens. I guess I'm just always kind of stressed? \n\nLike, take my foreman, Dave. He’s been doing this for fifteen years, and yesterday he completely messed up the framing on a load-bearing wall. I had to stand there in the mud—because of course it was pouring rain—explaining the blueprints to him like he was a first-year apprentice. And the whole time, the client is texting me non-stop. I swear, people watch one season of some home renovation show and suddenly they think they know more than the general contractor. They want custom arches but don't want to pay for the structural engineer to approve it. It’s wild. I actually had to turn my phone on silent yesterday just to hear myself think over the generators. \n\nBut, um... wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, if I'm upset right before I eat. Yeah, I don't know, I just haven't really tracked my mood like that. It's hard to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you feel better after a long or stressful day?\n\nPatient: I don't know if it makes me feel better, exactly. Maybe? Honestly, I don't really think about it like that, I just do it. It's kind of like... well, it's like when you're trying to fix a bad pour on a foundation, right? You just keep adding cement trying to level it out, but you're not really fixing the underlying issue, you're just making a bigger mess. \n\nSpeaking of messes, you wouldn't believe the site I was at this morning. The plumbers came in to do the rough-in for the master bath, and they completely ignored the updated schematics I sent them on Monday. I mean, I emailed them, I texted the lead guy, I even printed out a physical copy and taped it to the framing. And what do they do? They run the pipes right through where the custom vanity is supposed to go. Now I have to get them back out here, which is going to push the drywall guys back, and the client is already breathing down my neck about the timeline because they want to host some big Thanksgiving dinner in a house that currently doesn't even have walls. I just... I don't understand how people can be so bad at their jobs. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if eating makes me feel better. I haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after eating so much?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know if guilt is the right word. I guess I haven't really analyzed how I feel afterward, you know? I just feel... full. Uncomfortably full. \n\nBut honestly, the thing that makes me feel guilty is when I have to tell a client that their move-in date is delayed again. Like, I have this one couple right now, they're expecting a baby in two months, and they are so stressed about getting into the new house. And I had to sit them down yesterday and explain that the custom windows they ordered—the ones they absolutely *had* to have—are stuck on a cargo ship somewhere in the Pacific. I felt terrible, but what am I supposed to do? I can't build the windows myself. And then the husband started asking if we could just board up the openings temporarily so they could move in. I had to explain to a grown man why we can't just slap some plywood over a giant hole in the wall and call it a day, especially with a newborn. It's like people lose all common sense when they're building a house. \n\nSo yeah, dealing with that kind of stuff makes me feel guilty, I guess. But with the food? I don't know, I just haven't really thought about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started, or does it feel like you can't control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess that's the thing. Once I start on those evenings, it feels like I'm completely on autopilot. Like, I'm literally standing there telling myself to stop because I have an early crew start the next day, but I'm still going back to the kitchen for more. It's like I can't hit the brakes. \n\nWhich is ironic, because I spend my entire day telling people to hit the brakes. Like this morning, I had a guy on the excavator who decided he didn't need to wait for the utility locate flags to be marked before he started trenching for the new sewer line. I saw him from across the lot and had to literally sprint through the mud waving my arms like a maniac to get him to stop. I mean, do you know what happens if you hit a main gas line? Best case scenario, the whole neighborhood gets evacuated and we're fined thousands of dollars. Worst case, well, we're all blown to pieces. And he just looked at me from the cab like I was overreacting! I swear, the safety protocols on these sites are treated like suggestions half the time. If I wasn't there constantly breathing down their necks, they'd probably try to wire the electrical panels while standing in a puddle. \n\nBut yeah, with the food... it's like I'm the guy in the excavator, you know? I know I should stop, I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep digging anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a choice about whether or not to eat when you start eating on those evenings?\n\nPatient: I guess... no, I don't really feel like I have a choice. It's exactly like I said, I'm just on autopilot. I'll be standing right there in the kitchen, physically telling myself, \"You need to stop, you have to be on site at 5:30 AM to meet the concrete trucks,\" but my hands are just grabbing more stuff out of the pantry anyway. \n\nSpeaking of concrete trucks, you wouldn't believe the nightmare I had with the dispatcher yesterday. I ordered three trucks for a foundation pour, right? Specifically requested a 4000 PSI mix with a retarder because it was supposed to be 90 degrees out. Well, the first truck shows up an hour late, which means the crew is just standing around on the clock doing nothing. And then, when the driver finally hands me the ticket, I see they sent a standard 3000 PSI mix. No retarder. I had to reject the load right there on the spot. The driver was furious, the dispatcher was yelling at me on the phone, but what am I supposed to do? If I pour that mix in that heat, it's going to flash set before we can even get it screeded, and then I've got a ruined foundation that I have to pay to jackhammer out. It's like nobody pays attention to the details anymore. You have to double-check every single thing or people will just screw it up and expect you to deal with the fallout. \n\nBut yeah, with the eating... it just doesn't feel like a choice. I'm telling myself to stop, but I just keep going back for more.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens more often on days when you've had particularly stressful events at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, maybe? I haven't really tracked it like that. I guess I'm always stressed at work, so it's hard to tell if it's worse on certain days. \n\nLike, take yesterday for example. I had to deal with the electrical inspector. This guy, I swear he wakes up every morning and decides how he's going to make my life miserable. We had a rough-in inspection for a custom build, and he failed us because one of the outlet boxes in the kitchen island was a quarter of an inch too low according to some obscure local code amendment that they just passed last month. A quarter of an inch! I had the electrician right there, and he was ready to just rip the whole thing out and start over, he was so mad. I had to spend forty-five minutes calming him down and then another hour negotiating with the inspector to let us just adjust the bracket instead of rewiring the whole circuit. It's exhausting. You spend half your day just managing adult toddlers who throw tantrums when things don't go exactly their way. \n\nBut as for the food... I don't know if the stressful days make it happen more. It just kind of happens when it happens, I guess. I haven't really noticed a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens as a way to deal with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it's about the stress. I mean, I guess it could be? But like I said, I'm pretty much always stressed, so it's not like I can point to a bad day and say, \"Oh, that's why I ate.\" It just feels like it's something I do. \n\nHonestly, if I wanted to eat every time I was stressed, I'd never stop eating. Like, this morning, the framing crew showed up short two guys. Apparently, one of them threw his back out over the weekend, and the other one just didn't bother to call in. So suddenly I'm looking at a schedule that's already tight, and I've got half a crew trying to lift these massive LVL beams into place for the second-floor joists. I actually had to jump in and help them muscle one of those things up the ladders because we didn't have the crane scheduled until tomorrow. My shoulders are still killing me. And then, right in the middle of all that, the homeowner decides to drop by unannounced for a \"quick walkthrough.\" She's walking around in open-toed sandals, pointing at the framing and asking if we can just move a load-bearing wall two feet to the left so her kitchen island will have better flow. I had to smile and nod and explain that no, we can't just move a structural wall without getting an engineer to redraw the plans and pulling new permits, which would delay the project by at least three weeks. She looked at me like I was just being difficult on purpose. \n\nSo yeah, the stress is just... it's always there. I don't know if the eating is really about that or not. It's just something that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating helps you cope with the stress you experience at work?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if it helps. I mean, maybe in the exact second I'm doing it? But usually, I just feel terrible afterward, so it's not a very good coping mechanism if it is one. \n\nCoping is just... I don't know. You should see how some of my crew copes with stress. Like my lead drywaller, Terry. He copes by taking these ridiculous smoke breaks every forty-five minutes. I caught him yesterday out by his truck when he was supposed to be mudding the master bath, just chain-smoking and screaming on the phone to his ex-wife about child support. And I'm just standing there in the doorway, waiting for him to wrap it up so we can actually stay on schedule for the painters who are coming Thursday. \n\nWhich, by the way, the painters are a whole other headache. They always demand that the HVAC is running so the paint cures faster, but the HVAC guys haven't even installed the registers yet because the supplier sent the wrong finish. We specifically ordered brushed nickel, and they sent three boxes of oil-rubbed bronze. Who even uses oil-rubbed bronze anymore? It looks like it belongs in some 2005 McMansion. I literally had to load up forty heavy metal registers into the back of my truck and drive them back to the supply house myself, which killed two whole hours of my afternoon. \n\nSo... yeah, I'm not really sure. I guess I haven't really thought about whether the eating actually helps with any of that. It's just kind of a blur.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this immediately after a stressful event at work?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's like an immediate reaction, no. It's not like I get off a bad phone call and instantly go eat. It's more just... later, maybe? I don't really track the timing. \n\nSpeaking of timing, though, you wouldn't believe the scheduling nightmare I'm dealing with for the concrete pour on this new foundation. We were supposed to pour on Tuesday, right? But the meteorologist decides to predict a ninety percent chance of thunderstorms, so I have to call the concrete plant and push it to Thursday. Well, Thursday comes, and the weather is beautiful, not a cloud in the sky—but the pump truck breaks down on the highway. Just completely blows a transmission or something. So the concrete is sitting in the mixer trucks, getting hot, and the drivers are getting antsy because if that stuff sets up in the drum, they have to literally jackhammer it out. I spent three hours frantically calling every other pump company in a fifty-mile radius, begging someone to send a truck out. I finally found a guy, but he charged me double the standard rate just because he knew I was desperate. And then the homeowner was upset because the noise was bothering their neighbor's dog. A dog! We're pouring a foundation, of course it's loud. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know if the eating happens right after something stressful. I just haven't noticed if there's a connection like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat this way on days when nothing particularly stressful has happened at work?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know. Honestly, it's hard to even remember a day when nothing stressful happens. I guess maybe? But I couldn't tell you for sure. \n\nLike, even on days that are supposed to be \"easy,\" something always comes up. Take last Friday. We were just doing final punch-list items on a remodel. And of course, they had a dinner party planned for the weekend to show off the new kitchen. I... I really couldn't say if it's a reaction to being overwhelmed. I just haven't paid that much attention to it, to be honest. It's just something that happens. 

Overwhelmed is kind of my baseline anyway, you know? Like, you want to talk about overwhelmed, you should see the permitting office downtown. It's a total joke. I was down there on Monday trying to pull a simple electrical permit for a panel upgrade. Just a standard 200-amp service, nothing crazy. And the lady at the desk—I think her name was Brenda, she always wears these awful oversized reading glasses on a chain—she tells me I have the wrong form. I've been using this same form for six years! Apparently, they updated the building code last month and didn't bother to tell any of the contractors. So I have to stand there in the lobby, filling out this new ten-page packet on a clipboard with a pen that barely works, while three other guys behind me in line are sighing and checking their watches. And then, when I finally hand it back, she tells me the inspector is out sick all week anyway. Just... unbelievable. 

So, I don't know. I guess I don't really know if the eating is because I'm stressed or overwhelmed. It's just hard to tell when everything is always such a mess.
